SATURDAY, JULY 1, 1911.

THE WARRINGTON TRADES EXHIBITION. EMPIRE HALL WARRINGTON. September 28th to October 14th.

The Managers of the Empire Hall beg to Intimate to Manufacturers, Merchants and Retailers, that it is their Intention to organise a huge Trades Exhibition in the above Hall, which is the largest in Warrington and ranks as one of the finest in Lancashire. " Empire Hall " has a floor area without impediment or column of 18,000 square feet and possesses the most improved conveniences, having a fine Lounge Bandstand, Offices, Cloak Rooms and Electric Fitments so necessary for an ideal Exhibition.

The Musical engagements are unique and Include the celebrated Verona Ladles' Orchestra, as well as local singers and entertainers both Amateur and Professional. In addition many Side Shows of an amusing and pleasing nature have been secured to ensure a varied and complete change of Program each day during the full period of the Exhibition.

The whole arrangements surpass anything that has been previously attempted In Warrington. The Exhibition will afford the leading retail houses a long-wished-for opportunity of availing themselves of this unequalled form of publicity. For Plans and full particulars apply to Messrs SMITH & TURTON, Crampton's Chambers, Bridge Street, Warrington. Empire I fen '•

W V. TURTON, Secretary.

PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

(By Redcap.)

THE LAST CHAPTER.

As I stated in last week's issue, this will lie my concluding article for the present setoon, and further I promise.' to deal with the remaining matters uf the year in calendar form. The work done in the poultry world in July is almost a copy of what I advised for June so we will pass on to . .

Some of the early batched pullets will be commenc- ing to lay. DJ not force them ; keep them nicely on the move, hut under no consideration moat you give condiments. It is sometimes a temptation to encourage the early pullets to lay too rapidly. , Depend upon it, if you force them they will sutler for it in the long run. The seaside trade fir dressed poultry is now at its height and thove who cater for this clam should embrace every opportunity. Weed out all the cockerela not intended for stock purposes and feed them up so that you may realize the best prices. During this month you shou'il finally overhaul the sheds and sleeping quarters, give tin m an tittle crat of tar outside and a lime-wash inside. Repair all the fences where necessary and get your farm in good order while the weather is good and the days are long,

===== 0002601_19110701_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
srrinrlnt.

Generally epeaking. this is the month f the moult. Most of the old stook birds will now be heavy in moult. They should therefore be encouraged to ' get through as rapidly as possible. As they show signs of dropping the feathers, place them in a warm and dry place. Feed them liberally on oily foodstuffs Mit A little linseed meal in the morning's mash, and give them a good sprinkling if hemp-seed and sun- flower seed in the evening meal of grain. Thoroughly weed out the stock ; cockerels and pullets must be kept apart. Do not let the young pullets mix with old hens. This is also a good month to set duck eggs for the Christmas market, and also eggs from the best quality table fowls. Gather as ninny autunin leaves as you po-sibly can. They will come in handy for the scratching sheds during. the winter months.

You may now commence feeding the pullets more generously. Give them an occasional feed of cut green bone, awl when they do start to lay you may give them the grcen bone daily but do not over feed). Overhaul the foster-methers' and chickens' coop and thoroughly clean them before being stored nosy for the winter.

Beware of bad weather and feed accordingly. If very cold give the pullets a feed of maze in the after- noon. Keep the houses warm and dry and see that the fowl have plenty of dry litter to scratch among Always remember that dampness is absolutely deli- mental to egg-production. The morning mash cluring these cold months allude) be more tubstantial. A good mixture is as follows : Pea-meal, one part maize meal, one part ; third., one part ; to two parts biscuit meal. Live this mixtuto well scalded &IA dried off. Green food in plenty should be given at WOO. Don't forget the supply of sharp Ilitd grit. DE, EMBER. _ .

'The fowls and docks intended for the Christmas market should now he hurried en. Place them in a dry shed and feed them liberally three times a day. Oct your orders for these in good time no that you Carl better estimate p.m. operations. In feeding get cif possible) a good supply of skim-milk. This mined with ground oats, constitutes a good feeding mixture. , Do your best to produce good specimens, remembering ' that if you give satisfaction to new customers, you always retain them. Pay special attention to the pullets. I shall not be far out ill say now that eggs las per usual) will be :M. each in December and onwards. So that if you follow my previous remarks closely (and providing your stock is of a good laying strain.) you will get eggs in abundance. The most forward pullets could be reserved for an early breed- ing pen. If you intend hatching chicks in January, you will be obliged to mote up in December. There- fore you should select the earliest and best grown pullets for this purpose. If you mate up a pen if pullets port should riot mate them with a cockerel. Always mate a second year male bird with pullets. so that you may hope I,r rigorous offspring.

TIEC END.

And no now we come to the end. Many more new readers have been added to my long list during the present season, and I hr.pe that this method of dealing with the rest of the season in calendar form will prove helpful to them. It does not say that because these articles will not he wen for rime time to come that I shall be out of touch with yur. A postcard will always find me and if you are in difhicultiee I shall only be too pleased to help 300. Meanwhile, b,st wishes for sarcoma, and may you have a plem' supply of eggs at twopence each !

.„ ....... I have just received a copy of " l'ractical Poultry keeping for small-holders." This wonderful Lula book is written by two very rminent poultry-keepers. Messrs. J. Stephen hicks and Wilfrid 11. G. Ewart. Both these gentlemen are practical poultry-keepers, and in their book they houestly state the case of poultry-keeping for small-holders. The Look is Invaluable to small farmer.. It contains no over- drawn statements. There is nothing of the imaginary about it. It contains plain facts. A real honest attempt to assist the British farmer, in competition with foreign produce is what is chiefly aimed at. It is beautifully illusttated. It contains advice on bow to start, the beat brteds to go in F. r, general management, breeding, chicken rearing, egg production, disposing of produce, fatten- ing, the fancy side, ducks, turkeys, geese, disease remedies (simple), etc., etc. A rather unique idea is ihe addition of other branches of smell farming, such la pig keeping, fruit growing. sheep. tine and goats. Lastly it contains several chapters on the business side. Most valuable advice is given. I strongly recommend all my readers to obtain a copy of this most wonderfully cheap and concise treatise on •' Practical poultry keeping for amall-holders." Too can get it at low a figure as Oil., or post free for from the publishers, " The Feathered World," 9, Arundel-street, Strand, London, W.O This is the beat bit of advice I can give y u as I close my chapters tor this meson.

TANNING MATERIAL IN GERMANY.

Boomerang Effect of a Pro tective Duty.

Some interesting remarks on the effect of the ditties on quebrachn in Germany are given in the report of the trade of Bavaria for ItKi9 —lO by Mr. Ehrenracher, the British Coneul. In a debeG in the Baverian Chamber of Deputies, the question of the leather industry and the duty no quebracho wan raised. It was mentioned that the tariff on quebracho had not caused tie expected rise of prices of German oak bark rut the contrary, the liaverian leather induntry had suffered heavily, and therefore it seemed advisable to ennui the said tariff.

the duty,- it may be e—die-d,—;Cas about 35 per cent., and according to the Consul :- 1 "The consequence eves that the German leather industry was under the impreseion thst by showing a certain reserve it could force quebracho prices down• ward, and thue throw the duty rrn the shoulders of the Argentine exporters. lb. Argentine owner, of quehracho, however, found other markets, especially the United States of America. A rise in the quebracho market set in. To the German tanners the rise includ- ing the duty, mean. an enhanced seine of 100 per cent. In comparison with ths foreign competition, the German tanners have, therefore, been injured to, at leest, the extent of the duty. " In face of the present situation, it is considered that the Bavarian Government will now have to do its utmost to secure the abolition of the ditty, which, when introduced, was not intended to be a revenue duty, but a protective duty for Germanoak bark." The shove is instructive, and plainly shows the German tanners have their own specual set of difficulties to contend with in their efThrts to produce cheep leather from dear loideth—From the " Tennera' Tear Book."

THE REV. WILLIAM ROCKLIFF (Rector).

CLERGY OF THE CHURCH OF THE SACRED HEART.

The Church of the Sacred Heart, which serves the Itrman Catholic population of the Bank Quay district, has been a centre of great and constantly increruting usefulness since its erection in 1895 as the successor of a very unpretentious mission building. It 114 dignified and handsome edifice and its tall lower forms one of the most conspicuous landmarks ia the locality. The name of the Rev. Michael Ryan, who did such noble pioneer work, will ever he assuciated with the Church of the Sacred Heart, for it was he who instituted the original mission at a house in Lovely-lane in 1892 and gathered about hint such an earnest band of wurkers as to make the provision of a larger building a pressing necessity. The first removal was to an old works in Liverpool, 'ad on tEe ante note occupied by the extension of Messrs. Crosfield's great concern. At a very opportune time the Very Rev. Dean Lennan made an offer to build five churches in various parts of the Liverpool Diocese, and Warringt,n was one of the selected towns. Six thousard pounds were allocated to the erection of a church in the Bank Quay District, and thus it came about that the foundation atone of, the present Church of the Sacred Heart was laid, amid great rejoicinspr, on June 3rd, 1891, by the Very Rev. Monsignor James Carr, Vicar Captular. In the meantime the lick-up shop, which is now the club room of the Ycung Men's Society, was taken for mission purposes until the scheme could be completed. The new church was opened with great solemnity in June, 1895, by Bishop Whiteside, of Liverpool, and its progress up to the present day has been highly encouraging to all who have devoted themselves to the advancement of the good work there. After seventeen years of devaed service the Rev.M. Ryan left Warrington towards the end of 190'.),t, become Rector of the Church of Our Lady Star of the Se r .St. Anne's-

Use and Misuse ot Coronations.

At Kennington Chapel, Liverpcol, on Sunday, the Rev. 11. Duanico, formerly of Warrington, in thfi course of his sermon criticised in drastic terms what he called the unreality of the Coronation. It fostered the foolish idea that monarchy mated upon force of arms, Wan it not en °wrap that in that procesxion which wended Re way from the Palace to the Abbey there was not a single representative of religion, art, scion., tradm commerce or civic government, bat just one 101 l glorification of wan? Such • proceesion was more in keeping with a Pagan than professedly Christi. nation. It wane]. a misuse of the Coronation to entrant it exclusively to one of the many sects in this co.ntry, for that was all the Church that called itself the Church of England was. Evan in F:ngland that Church repro- mated not more than one-half the religious people, whereas in Wales, Scotland end Ireland it represented hot • mail minority. The mine might be said of it In the vast Dominion. .ross the sena Why should one Church out of many exclusively take charge of a cere- mony that should demonstrate the religions as well as the political unity of the Empire? The Coronation was also misused in so fern, it diverted the attention of the people from the artusi reelitim. It was Redi to keep in mind that behind all the military display, the glittering •rray of coronets and coatiy robee, the snobbery of caste and privilege, there were burnsa creatures of divine origin wallowing in the ab.s, hungry wretches stArving on life's highways, millions toiling without hope at the wheel of labour, and little children dying like flies in reeking slums If they did remember them things they might ask whether they were justified in holding costly festivals end wasting hard-earned money.

, WAR RINGTON Church /New.

IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

CAN THE LORDS FORCE AN ELECTION?

The Coronation and the splendour of its celebraiions now being over and gone we are promised some political developments in which Ireland and the Irish are most closely interested. After flamboyant orations by Mr. Balfour on Imperial politics, the minds of the Tories it is said have been busily occupied with the fate and destiny of the Veto Bill. Wrecking tactics are to be adopted whilst the echo of Mr. Balfour's lecture on party, peace, and blessedness still lingers in our ears. It has been rumoured first of all,that the Peers will pass the Veto Bill subject to a reference being made to the people on the point of self- government for Ireland. This was alleged to come from a well authenticated source and we learned yesterday in a political club in London of considerable importance, that a General Election might be forced by the end of next month. Whilst this is not only far- fetched but quite outside the bounds of possibility even in the uncertain sphere of politics, it is however perfectly certain that hurried and important conferences between the Unionist leaders have been held during the past few days, and it is now publicly announced that a wrecking policy is to be adopted in the Lords. Amend- ments have been framed with a view to the Veto Bill being shorn of its constitutional reforms and for the further and main purpose of excluding Home Rule from the scope of its operations. The plain English of all this indicates a position which no body of self respecting men would consent for an instant to be forced into, and it is not difficult to imagine what Mr. Asquith and Mr. Lloyd George and that other splendid fighter, Mr. Winston Churchill, will say in reply to the suggestion that they should thus jettison the central themes and the leading principles of modern Liberalism. Happily the mind of the Government with regard to Ireland is well and clearly understood. But if it were not, had we notffiad leading pronouncements by Mr. Asquith and all his prominent colleagues in the Cabinet we should have no hesitation in declaring that this latest manifestation of Toryism in a temper would not disquiet the minds of the men in Britain and Ireland who sent the present Government back to West- minster to finish the work they had com- menced of giving the House of Commons its due and rightful place in the affairs of the country. The Nationalist position has, of course,been put with such frequent and lucid exposition by responsible Nationalists that the least alert must needs know what that position is. We accept Mr. Asquith's Albert Hall declaration, we have good faith in his considered utterances representing Cabinet policy which he made during the recent election, and we are at present throwing in our political strength—a supreme factor as things balance out to-day—on the clear understanding that those pledges, and not a jot less, shall be redeemed. So that we appear to be approaching a season of exceed- ingly high politica: interest.

===== 0002601_19110701_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HOME RULE CAMPAIGN.

We were informed by Mr. Redmond the other day that the arrangements for the ' Home Rule campaign to be carried on in the constituencies of Great Britain were now almost completed. Not even in the height of th 3 enthusiasm of Mr. Gladstone's strenu- ous campaigns was the interest and the zeal greater than that with which the Liberal forces throughout the country are to-day evincing in the cause of self Government for Ireland. Applications for speakers pour in daily and an equal demand is being made for propaganda literature. The campaign I proper will not open until about October and then there is expected to he a host of meetings in almost every part of Great Britain. Mr. Dulanty in response to requests from the young Liberals, of Manchester, Salford and district, has secured Mr. Joseph Devlin, M.P., for the monstre demonstration which is to be held in Alexandra Park, Manchester, and at which Mr. William Jones, M.P., junior Liberal Whip is also to speak. A big demand for tickets is already being made. The Liberal organisations of Warrington and district would do well to consult the local Nationalists, Councillor Owen Toole and others, with a view to linking up Nationalist support and knowledge on this now promi- nent question.

===== 0002601_19110701_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GAELIC LEAGUE.

Spenser, the English poet who knew Ireland perhaps better than any Englishman in the Tudor period, said with reference to the native Irish and their language that "if the tongue be Irish the heart too must needs ho Irish." Anything therefore which helps the Irish language movement helps to keep the heart of Ireland preserved in the strength of its own distinctive nationality. With this idea before their minds, the Gaelic League authorities of South East Lancashire availed themselves of the Coronation Day to hold in deridheacht (open-air festival) in Whalley Range, Manchester, where addresses were delivered by Mr. (YRyan, Mr. J. W. Dulanty and Rev. C. McCarthy. Irish. songs in the old tongue, Irish music and the traditional Irish dances were given by the members of the Gielic League and a truly Irish day was much enjoyed by the large crowd that gathered there. CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB

HOW SURGERY WON AN ELEPHANT'S AFFECTION.

- - - - In the Zoological Garden at Asakusa Park Tokio,Japan, there lives an elephant. He k a wild lumbering beast, but sensible withal. and knows his friends. At times, too, feeling himself aggrieved, lie has attacked and injured seven men, but one man, a veterinary surgeon, can now handle him at will. And it all comes about through surgical aid in time of need that was extended by the surgeon.

„... The elephant. in getting up from the floor where he slept one night, caught his eyelid on a nail, and made an ugly wound, from which hung shreds of the eyelid. The surgeon tells what happened. The story is related by a missionary, who knows the circumstances. The surgeon's narrative is as follows :

"Twos called upon to treat the elephant, and although I felt much afraid of the big beast, as I had heard how wild he was. I determined to attempt it. First of all. I took ropes and endeavoured to fasten his trunk to an iron bar, but my efforts were out at once. "As this plan failed, I knew it would be impossible to treat him by force, but a happy thought came to me. What do you think it was? 1 will tell you. I called to the elephant's keeper, stood him h. front of the elephant, and told him to pretend his eyes were sore by some gesture. I then treated his eyes and bandaged them, and he went away. This 1 repeated three times, whilethe elephant stood quietly watching. After the third treatment I took off the bandage, washed the keeper's eyes, and told him to pretend he was quite well of his sore eyes. He pretended to be very grateful, thanked me many times, and went away. Then with a strong determination I went up to the elephant, and putting a ladder against his big body I climbed to his back. With a pair of sharp scissors I tried to cut off the piece of torn eyelid, hut as I began cutting, the elephant screamed very loudly. I had never heard such a noise, and I was filled with fear in every nerve. I sprang to the floor, and backed away trembling in every limb. On looking up I saw the scissors still dangling from the elephant's eyelid. I then climbed up again very carefully, and summoning all my strength I cut off the piece of eyelid, this time succeeding also to treat him as I desired. Then I washed his eyes. I went every day for some time to treat and wash his eyes, and be soon became so grateful and fond of me to treat him. Thus I succeeded by strategy where force had failed. I visit the elephant once in two weeks, and he still knows me, and looks upon me as his friend. TIIE BOY AND THE BIRD. " (II weed in the ;olden till after ten." PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The programme submitted at the Palace and Hippodrome this week has many merits. Needless to ' my, "The Cattle Thief," presented by ( leorge Street and Company, stands out prominently above the rest of the turn a, n it only on account of its magnitude, but because of Mt original conception. A realistic picture of life oat West is given, its value being greatly enhanced by an ingebiously conceived plot. The most powerful actor amongst his seven or eight well-chosen colleagues is Mr. Street himself, who amongst other things admirably sustains the play by Iris I,amorous independence. As • confidential comedian Robb Wilton is grand, the story of his career son fireman being very funny, whilst at the same time the tone retains • high level. An item of strong interest is the Coronation film. It in a real bestity end provokes much admiration. The procession receives adequate attention and the magnificence of the Royal carriage with Their Majesties plainly •isible inside creates as indelible impression. This film e.14111b1 any previous triumph of cinematography. As a ventriloquist and whistler Richard Taylor is very entertaining, his concern. tine with his doll being particularly clever. The Prince Milner Trio introduce some characteristic humour into a capital acrobatic act Some of the feats evince considerable dexterity and the audience do not fail to appreciate them. The Late Ir. Harry Berrey.

The Si. Helens Monica' Society on Tneeday night gave sconcert at the St. Helene Town Hall under the direction of Dr. Bidden for the benefit of the widow of the late Mr. Harry Barmy, the well-known choir- master who died at an early age • abort time ago at Warrinwoo. Mr. Berrey was • St. Helene man, and ban been particularly succeesful in developing local talent and training various choirs for competitions and other contests. The programme comprised only the music of British composers, and included Stan- ford's " Revenge " ; Fletcher's prize chorus. " For Empire and King"the recessional "God of our Father.," and Sir Edward Elgar's " Land of Hope end Glory." There was a large attendance. The band and chorus numbered over 150. and the soloists had been specially selected from local singers by Mr. Harry Evans, F.R.(10., coodoctor of the Liverpool Welsh Choral Union.

THE CRAZE FOR PLEASURE.

HOW IT IS AFFECTING EDUCATION IN WARRINGTON.

Important Report to the Education Committee.

There is still another outside influence which is very greatly affecting the work In the schools, and that hi the present day tendency for indulgence in pleasure. The committee will be aware that, during the last few }We, there has been. Very large increues in planes of arcumment, which cater, -• .

at -a very low pro., for children, and the first house, in the evening at these places of entertain- ment ere attended very largely by children. The above statement appear. in an important report on the teaching of arithmetic In the elementary schools of Warrington which was presented by the Director of Eduostion (Mr. J. Moore hlurray) to the Education Committee at their monthly meeting on Monday. Tbe report was • most exhaustive one and treated largely upon general educational topics as well as upon the specific queetion of the teaching of ' arithmetic. We give the following estrsots it is now generally agreed that

KOOO NATIONS ARK I.NOKSIRARI.I6 for children tinder 12 years of age' and extremely ! !undesirable if they are to be made• teet of the efficiency ! of • school. In compering the preeent generation of scholarn with preceding generation., It must be remem- I dared that little more than one generation of children bare paned through the '<hoc's under the new eyetem, a child's school life being eight years, and it was only in 1900 that the examinations ceased and the methods adopted in the schools began to change. Conspirators In the act of conspiring

FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M.P.

AMENDING THE OLD AGE PENSIONS

ACT. SOME PROSPECTIVE PERSONS IN DANGER.

While the public interest is absorbed in considering the Insurance Bill or waiting for the result of the contest between the Peers and the Commons, there is every reason to fear that a Bill will be allowed to pass into law which will deprive tens of thousands of old people of the pensions to which they have been looking forward, and to which they will be entitled on reaching the age of 70, as the law is at present. Under the pretext of " amending " the present Act in certain particulars, it is proposed in a Bill which was read a second time last week in the House of Commons to deprive all persons in the future, of the right to a pension if they have been so inconsiderate as to save a iittle sum of money to keep them from the workhouse in their old-age. At present, the amount of the pension varies from ss. to is. a week according to the means of the applicant. A person who has an income of £3l 10s. or more is not entitled to a pension, but one who has less than £2l is entitled to a full pension of is. a , week. If this Bill be passed no person in future will be granted the old ago pension who has money or property beyond £3lO in I value. At present the actual income of a person is taken as the basis, but in future it is proposed that the actual income shall be disregarded altogether, and the income of a person shall be regarded as one-tenth of the capital value of the property of the applicant. This means that hundreds of thousands of old people in the future will either get no pension at all or will get one much less than they would do if the law remained as it is now.

===== 0002601_19110701_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
A TYPICAL CASE.

Let us take one or two typical cases. A married couple, during the time their grown up children were at home, managed to save say £3OO which is invested in a house. This house will bring in say a rent of about ss. a week. This is not enough to keep either one or both of the old people. At present the possession of this small income does not deprive a person of the right to the full pension. But under this Bill the income of the owner will be taken at one-tenth of the value of the house, and in censequenee no pension can be obtained. Any sum above £2lO will reduce the pension below the sum which is given to-day, and although the income may not be more than 2s. a week, this new Bill will take it at five or six times that figure. Whatever the Government are thinking about to bring forward such a proposal as this passes comprehension. It is taking away from old people the pension to which they I had been looking forward, and which was guaranteed to them by the existing Act. At the General Election before last a certain member of the Govern- ment made himself notorious by circulating the report that Old Age Pensions would be in danger if there were a change of Govern- ment. The leaders of both parties hastened to assure the old folks that their pensions were as safe as the Bank of England, and now there is actually a Government proposal in a Bill to take away the pension rights from one very large and worthy section of the old people. It is an amazing proposal, and one which only requires to be realised to arouse a general storm of protest in the land. This Bill is brought in ostensibly to remove certain hardships in the existing Act. It deals with the case of widows of aliens who themselves are British born subjects ; also

with the point that, even since the Poor Law disqualification was removed the husband is still disqualified for a pension if the wife is in receipt of Poor relief. It also deals with the hardships which have been inflicted by the regulations as to residence out of country. In none of these matters does the Bill redress the grievance thoroughly. In the first mentioned instance it proposes that where a British born woman who has married an unnaturalised foreigner is left a widow, or has been deserted by her husband, or is legally separated from him, she shall, if otherwise qualified, be entitled to get the old ago pension. There have been a number of cases where old women, who have never been out of Great Britain, and who have been widows for many years, have been refused the pension because their dead husbands were foreigners. Such women, when the Bill passes, will be able to get the pension. But there will still be the disqualification on the wife of an alien who is in all other respects qualified.

===== 0002601_19110701_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
RESIDENCE ABROAD.

The strict regulations framed by the Treasury under the powers given to them by the Old Age Pensions Act as to residence in the United Kingdom have deprived a very large number of persons of the Old Age Pension. Under the Act a person must have had his home in this country for the twenty years before he claims a pension. This has been construed very harshly. Persons, for instance, who have lived for a little while in the Isle of Man have been disqualified. It is proposed in the new Bill to allow eight yearn absence from the country during the twenty years preceding the application for a pension. Even this will still leave many cases of hardship. It will be possible for a person to have spent sixty years of his life in this country, and still on attaining the age of 70 to be deprived of a pension because he has been away, say ten years in the Colonies. It is proposed in this Bill to relax some- what the hard condition in regard to imprisonment. Under the present Act a person who has been sent to prison for any offence without the option of a fine is dis- qualified for ten years from having an old age pension. This is cruel, and vindictively pursues a man who has already paid a Revere penalty for his sins. It is now proposed to reduce the period of disqualification to two years. Even this is too hard. Many a man is sent to prison without the option of a fine for an offence to which there is no moral turpitude attached, and very often he is wrongfully imprisoned. The imprisonment should purge the offence, and the Old Age Pensions Act should not punish an old man or woman of 70 by the additional penalty which deprivation of pension inflicts. The great blot of this amending Bill is the proposal which has been dealt with which proposes to penalise thrifty old people. It has been introduced into the Bill no doubt to get back what the increased cost of removing the other disqualifications will involve. But there is a petty meanness about such an action which the Government of a rich country like ours should not commit. There are some reforms of the existing Act which it is not proposed to embody in this new Bill It has always been felt to be a grievance that the pension which a man gets from a friendly society or a trade union should count against him for the purpose of an old age pension. This is left untouched by the new Bill. Instead of proposing to ignore such income as that, the Bill is suggesting that all forms of thrift shall be capitalised and translated into terms of annuity value. This is something like a workman complain- ing that he did not get as much wage as his mate, and the employer equalises matters by reducing his mate's wage. ANOTHER HARDSHIP.

One other matter should have been dealt with in the new Bill. It is the custom of the Pension officers, acting under the instruc- tions of the Treasury, to estimate a profit from the sums which are paid by children

to their parents for board and lodgings. In some cases the amount of the estimated profit is absurd, and in many instances pensions have been lost or reduced by this means. There really is no profit in such cases. I know one case where the pension authorities took two shillings a week as the profit made by the mother out of the sum paid by her daughter for board and lodgings. And in this case the daughter paid seven shillings a week I All these difficulties would disappear if we had a universal system of old age pensions. Any partial scheme will always be full of anomalies and instances of unfairness. The old age pension should not be given on account of poverty, any more than the pension of a Government official is so given. It should be a State pension for the years of service the recipient has spent in the t;ervice of his country in some industrial occupation. That is the only fair way of doing, and all the Commissions and Committees whic'a have enquired into this subject have had to come to the same conclusion. It is only the cost of a universal scheme which stands in the way of its adoption. It is always the cost, when the workers' claims are pressed.

CORONATION DAY AT WOOLSTON.

It was unfortunate that the most interesting festival which had been arranged by the -Woolston Coronation Committee was marred by incessant rain on Saturday. After the distribution of medals and the singing of "God save the King "a procession started from Martinscroft Green, headed by Mr. A. W. Howard as marshal, mounted, and in the uniform of a dragoon. Miss Greta Howard followed, also mounted, in the character of the Duchess of Devonshire. Four members of the Legion of Frontiersmen in uniform of khaki and slouched hat, preceded the band of the Padgate Cottage Homes, and the children of Woolston C.E. and Martinscroft R.C. Schools followed. A number of children were daintily attired as Japanese. Among the adults' fancy dress costumes were representations of Red Cross nurses, daffodils, sunflowers, winter and summer, and suffragettes. A result of "votes for women' was pictured. One of the most con- spicuous and effective characters was Britannia, sustained by Miss A. Dennett, with Union Jack shield, and trident in hand, The Cadishead Band headed the Ancient Order of Foresters, and lastly were Boy Scouts and lurries containing young children. Owing to the rain a shorter route was taken than had been originally planned. Tea was served, first to the children, and then to adults, in a marquee at 5 p.m. Selections of music by the band occupied the time until eight o'clock, and an impromptu concert in St. Peter's Schoolroom followed. The sports were postponed until July Ist.

A STRANGE CONSPIRACY.

By GUY BOOTRBY,

Author of .9, Bid for Fortune,' "Dr. Nikola," "Beautiful White Devil," "Fascination at the King," "Pharos the Egyptian," am

There are some people in this world, I know, -who would tell you that when all is said upon the subject, • pretty girl is, after all, only • girl. But the most prosaic person could not have denied the fart that Ethelwyn Caretairs was not only • remarkably pretty girl, but • decidedly accomplished one to boot She had, moreover, • pretty wit. Bob (I never particularly liked Bob C-uatairs until I fell desperately in love with hid sister) used to say that "the man who thinks he's going to score off Wyn will find himself in Queer-street before she's done with him." Taking the risk of allowing it to be supposed that she MS that most odious of all human creatures "a paragon," I must put it on mood that she rode straight to hounds, danced well, played tennis alined up to champion form, and hod well-nigh vs good an eye for • horse as her father, which :1 saying something—considering that the old squire is usually to be found among the judges at the principal shows. As for her intellectual attainments—well, I'm afraid I don't know very much shout them, save that she plays the piano well enough to please me, and that, like most of her sex, she enjoys a good novel—mine, I hope, for preference. Old Carstairs, or, to give him his due, Sir renniston Care:airs, B sronot, of Egford Hall, in the South Riding, is, or was—for he also has now joined the G mat Majority—ft gentleman of the old school, sueh as is seldom met with nowadays. Ito kept open house, believed in upholding Church and State, and would as noon have thought of omitting morning prayers—at which every retainer from butler to stable-boy eumpolled to attend—as he would of giving • polcher, caught red-lianded, half-a-crown for his proficiency in the study of animal life. Lady ^Carstaire was small, grey-haired, and motherly, and when you have said that you have said every- thing. Thera remsins only Mr. Re`iert Winstanley *Carruthers Carntairs, who at the time of which I write was just recovering from the effects of flying sent down from his college for "riotous and altogether unseemly behaviour." He was a personable youth, hut with a predilection for •sporting waistcoats and clay pipes, which latter only appeared to become valuable as they neared the end of their esintence. So much for the Cantab.. family. Now for the house and property, and then to my story with all despatch. The Hall itself was • fine old building, dating from the reign of the eighth Henry, full of quaint rooms and passages, secret staircases and cup- board; and I have beard it said a monk's room, which could only be approached by climbing up the dining-room chimney. There are as many as three ghoits, I believe, to say nothing of one, a cavalier, who rides round the noble old deer park on a grey home on the anniversary of the death of the White King. Taken •Itogether, it is aa noble a property as anyone could wish to possess, and if Sir Robert Winstanley Carruthers Carstairs, Baronet, is not proud of it—well, •11 I can say is, he ought to be ducked in his owe trout stream and left to crawl out by himself. First and foremost do not run away with the idea that lam the hero of thin story. I would that I had been—but that fickle jade Fortune willed otherwise. lam simply the hietorian whe records the facts as they occurred, and who declines to be held responsible for minis details. The trouble—and bad trouble I eau assure you it was— arose out of the fact that young Carstairs had got into the wrong set at college. Being an Englishman, and • Yorlinhireinan in particular he liked sport, but unfortunately not always sport of the legitimate kind; dog and cork fighting, badger drawing, and similar little aimmements seemed to give him greater pleasure than anything else. l'igeon shooting occupied a consideralaht 'portion of his spare time, while some of the -amounts he was well known to bass lost at cards made many • respectable young undergraduate's hair stand on eant with horror. The squire was , -liberality itself, bgt even his allowance went monitors, so to put matters right Neater Bob gal Into debt to a fine round figure. beart until that redoubtable o-ai'eioi', Major Willoughby Norris, of the 3rd something or t • other, appeared upon the scene and captured it by wenlade, direct assault, or whatever the term , 'may be. What she could see in him, if his fame were pat oil one side, none of us could under- ' Adana. He was the youugeet Major on the Army list at that time, so I I was informed, and received some decoration or another for_his gallantry in holding a fort or I something of the kind in a place on the top of I India, the name of which I have never been able la remember. Please don't think jealous, tor I assure you I'm nothing of the sort. But I don't mind confessing that girls like Ethelwyn ' Carstairs arc not to be met with every day. It seems that old Carstaire was a great friend of Norris's father, and be renewed acquaintance with the eon, to whom he bad acted as godparent, at a club in town soon after the young man's return to England. The visit lasted a fortnight, and at the end of that time the engagement was •innounecd. He then went off on a round of gamily visits, and when he had worked them off weturned to the Hall. It was delight- ful summer weather, and the old haulm and the park were looking their very best. My favourite! cob was still in the land of the living, and when on the morning following my arrival Jenkins, the head-keeper, found occasion to inform me that the fishing bad never bees better, well, I heaved a sigh of contentment., and felt that my lot had indeed fallen in pleasant places. One lovely morning I was writing letters in the billiard-room ; the squire had gone down to tile home farm, the Major and his riaarre were wandering about the shrubberies, while Lady Carstairs was interviewing the housekeeper in the morning-room. Presently the door opened, and the heir of the house entered. He pretended Out he did not know I was in the room, but I was not deceived by his apparent ignorance. Like loony other people who try the same thing, ha overdid it, He wandered to the mantelpiece, took up an ornament, put it down again, whistled o few bars of a popular melody, and then tuned round and said: .1 asy, I'm awfully sorry, Uncle Jiin. I didn't see you. I'm afraid I muss ham disturbed Yew' "Net at ll my boy. Not at all. I replied, "I've just finished my letters. What as the order of the day ? Fishinj, 491 4 t

Here was a nice position for a man to find himself placed in. However, I listened; all came out, and a pitiful tale it was. He had given his father a list of his debts, but, like a young idiot, he left out the biggest and ugliest of them all—particulars of which by no means redounded to his credit, The woman—for it was a woman— was dunning him for the money, and he dared not tell his father and ask him for more. As a matter of fact, she had even threatened to come herself and tell everything. You will agree the situation was a difficult one. The claim was outrageous, but something had to be done if a scandal were to be avoided. My advice to him was, "Go to your father, make a clean breast of it, and I think you'll find bell help you out of it. But don't shilly-shally. Tell him to-day, and be done with it.

... "Very well," he said, with a face as white as a sheet. "But he'll never forgive mo." Then he went out. _ .

I confess I was sorry for the boy, and I hoped he would keep his word. I presumed he had, for when next I met the squire I found him unusually depressed—so much so, indeed, that he went to his study and shut himself in instead of going fishing with me as arranged. Such a thing it is to have a family, I told myself. Next day the squire seemed a little more cheerful, but not so his son. ghat young gentle- man went about with a face like that of a pro- fessional mute. As he said nothing to me on the subject I did not refer to our conversation of the previous day. That evening he did not put in an appearance in the billiard-room after dinner on the plea that he had a headache and. was going to bed. As a matter of fact, we were all early birds, and, no far as I could tell, the house was locked up by eleven. - - - • •

How long I had been asleep I cannot say, but suddenly I found myself being violently shaken by the shoulder. With a grunt (expressive. I have no doubt, of disapproval) I rolled over, opened my eyes, and asked what was the matter. It was daylight, and to my astonishment my visitor was no less a person than the Major, and, stranger still, he was fully dressed; but not aa

I hacrfast seen him. Nov he worn an outdoor knickerbocker suit and carried • cap in his bond. "Anything wrong ? " I asked, not being able to understand the reason of his visit.

"It's just three o'clock. Dress yourself quickly and I'll tell you, was hi. reply.

I tumbled out, began to dress, and while I did so he told me his tale.

"Look here, Braithwaite," he said, "you're an old friend of the family and I know that I'm safe in confiding in you. There's the deuce and all to pay. You remember that young Bob did not slew up in the billiard-room after dinner last night, but said he was going to bed. Well, I happen to know that he did go to his room, but only to change his clothes, after which he went out, returning about half.past nine. An hour , and a-half ago I heard a step on the gravel ' below my window, and, wondering who it could be at such an hour, I looked out. The moon was still shining and I could see that it was Bob, with a bag in his hand, making his way through the shrubberies towards the side gate. Evidently he was bound for the station. Huomng there was trouble in the wind, I dressed as quickly as I could and went after him, but I was just too late to catch him. The train left the platform eel reached it."

"This is bad—very bad," I answered. "I don't mind telling you in confidence that the foolish lad is in serious trouble. To run away will only be to make matters worse." My companion groaned as if he were in actual pain. "A woman, do you say ? Great Heavens, my suspicions were correct after all, then! Oh, his poor family!" "What do you mean ?" "Liden. After I left the station I went for a walk to think matters over. It was just growing light I entered the park by the south lodge and made my way along the riverside until I came to that small copso at the bend. Why I should have entered it I do not know, but I did so, to dis- cover a woman's body lying among the bracken. She was dead—had been strangled as a matter of fact, for the rope was still round her neck. 1 came back as fast aa I could to the house to consult with you as to what should be done."

We looked at each other in horror. The game thought was uppermost in both our minds. I was the liret to speak. His family most know this morning that he has gone. The body will be found and the police communicated with—and then what ? "

"Don't—don't—for pity's sake! We must wave them and him at any hazard. It would break 'Ethelwyn's heart."

twos thinking more of the unfortunate father and mother. "You must come with me and see the woman," he said at last. "We must then talk it over and try to see what can be done." I followed, him meekly from my item and down the great, silent house. Once in the hall below he stopped end turned the key of the dining-room door. Entering he mixed two brandies and sodas, and gave me one. "You will want it," he said, shortly. Then we set off on our ghastly errand, talking am we went. We found the body, and my suspicions were confirmed, for in the pocket of the jacket was the return half of a railway ticket from Cambridge. "Put that in your pocket and burn it when you get home," said the Major; and I obeyed. Having recovered from my first feeling of horror I looked more closely at the unfortunate woman. Distorted though her face was she was not unattractive, and at one time might even have been pretty. She was dressed in a flaehy style; also wore a hat with trailing feathers. She wore a wedding ring, and •half-hoop of imitation diamonds on the third finger of the right hand. There was a purse in her pocket containing twenty-five shillings and sixpenee, and, what added to our horror, a paoket of letters from that wretched young Bob, all signed with his full IMMO. -

eeee, "Put those in your pocket, too," ordered the Major. "We'll burn them unread." - - - .

Again I obeyed, and asked what we should do nest. - - - "-

"We must find some safe place to bide the body for the present," he said; "somewhere ' where no one will ever think of looking for it. Do you know of any such place?" ' I thought and thought, but could not recall any that would answer our purpose. At last an idea occurred to me. There was an old ice-house at the end of the lake. It had not been opened for years. No one would ever think of looking there. "Come along, thee, • said the Major; and though I loathed the work I obeyed. "Heaven help us if anyone should see us!" he added, as we bore our grisly burden along in the bright morning light. But no one did see on. We reached the house safely, entered it, and laid the body on the floor in the darkest part. Then we left, carefully closing the door behind us.

"We must find some nails and a hammer and fasten that door up as soon as possible," said the Major as we stode back to the house. I agreed with him. On reaching the house we made haste to destroy the incriminating railway ticket, and , that done felt more at ease. But how were we to account for Bob's mysterious departure? That would have to be explained somehow. "Could you forge a letter from him to his mother, do you think?" asked the Major, anxiously. "If I could I'd do it like a shot." "I've never tried such a thing," I answered. "But if you like I'll do no now. Tell me what I am to say." I spread one of the wretched youth's letters to . . .

the woman out on the table before me for a copy, and waited for him to begin. We knew it must ,be expressed in lds own peculiar plaraseolou. The result was as follows:

"Dear Ntater —l've cot no end of • }lrma. Camp, so I to on by the early trai, to town for a week or so. Square the governor.—Your loving son, "Boa*

II I wrote one copy I moat have written 0

couple of dozen before I was aatisfled. When It was at last completed I could have wept over it and the thoughtu it conjured up. The only thing the Major could lay was "Poor Ethelwyn!" Theo we burnt the letters as we had done the ticket, and Norris picked up the one I had , written. "I'm going to slip this quietly under Lady Carstalrs' bedroom door," he said, 'so thnt she may get it before she meeta on. Heaven bless you, Braithwaite; you've proved yourself a true Ifriend. I'm afraid I should not have had the pluck to go through with it alone." "Bosh!" I answered. "You've done more than I have." I ran tell you, and with truth, that never is my life have I spent such another day as that. What I cannot understand is why I did not go off my heed. When Lady Carstaire came down to breakfast she informed on that her son had gone up to town, and added the little fib that he begged us to forgive him. I thought I should have broken down entirely; indeed, it vats only the Major's sly kick that made me remember what was expected of me. Then the squire put in an appearance and had something to say on the subject, after which came Miss Ethelwyn, who, sister like, spoke her mind plainly and to the point. How the wretched Rob felt I cannot Rey, but I know that I went about like a murderer on the eve of his execution. I could not settle to any- thing, and felt a cold shiver run over me every time the front-door bell rang. Norris nod dared scarcely meet each other's eyes, but later in the day he found occasion to inforos me that he had nailed the ice-house door up--which was something to be thankful for. That night I did not sleep a wink, but sat in my armchair reading until sunrise. It was destined to be an eventful

day. After breakfast Ethelwyn suggested that her lover should take her on the Joke to pick water- lines, and to her surprise he begged to be excused on the plea that he had a touch of fever, the result of his Indian campaign, and thought it prudent to remain at home. I was afraid she was going to ask me to take her, but she did not. I would not have gcno near the lake fur anything. That afternoon the squire and his lady drove off to pay a call some seven or eight miles away. Miss Etliclwyn was due at a village meeting, so that the Major and 1 were left to our own

Between four o'clock and half-past we were sitting in the cool of the great Hall, not doing much in the way of conversation, as you may suppose, when the Major suddenly sprang to his feet with a cry. I followed his example in both respects, and you will be able to understand why when I tell you that, entering by the front door, Gladstone in hand, was no lees a person than young Robert, the very man of all others who should have been hundreds of miles away. The position had suddenly become worse than ever.

"You young idiot I" cried Norris, in a whirl- wind of wrath. "Are you mad that you venture back here ? "

"Mad ?" said the other, in astonishment, "Why should 1 be? I've an good a right here as any- one else. What do you mean ? " Though he spoke with assurance his face had suddenly grown deathly pale. He knew that we held his eecret. "Hush!" I whispered. "Don't talk here, come to the billiard-room." He followed on there, still carrying his bag. I closed the door carefully. "I'm not going to tell you what I think of you," began the Major, "for your people's sake. But this I do tell you, that your coming back here is either sheer madness on your part, or selfish consideration for your own feelings an opposed to theirs. Why, man, the police may be here for you at any moment. We found the woman--" "The woman ?" he cried, and staggered back against the table as if he were shot, At that moment Etlielvvyn passed the window. "Come!" I cried, frantically. "Run for your life and bide yourself upstairs. Let no one see you if you value your mother's love. We will tell you everything later. Run, run 1 You haven't an instant to lose." There was a second door from the room, and through this he dashed. Instinctively we picked up cues and pretended to play. A few moments later Ethelwyn entered, looking prettier than I had ever seen her in her delightfully cool summer frock. She glanced round the room as if in surprise. "Why, where is Bob ?" she asked. "Bob?" we ejaculated simultaneously. Then Nurrie added: "Why, in London surely? "But I saw him in here as I passed the window. Don't tease me, dear. Has he come home?" "My dear girl, I have already said that he is in London. Did he not tell your mother in hie letter that he would not be back for a week ? " "But 1 saw him here. I am sure of it." To what extent we perjured ourselves I dare not think, but even at the end she waa not convinced. That idle still believed we were deceiving her I could see, and she wan propor- tionately angry with us both, her lover in particular. Their first quarrel was imminent. I wonder what she would have said had she known the truth. We were saved, however, by the fact that just at the right moment the squire and his wife returned, and we nll adjourned to the Hall for tea. Three of on at least did nut feel inclined for it. We had not been seated in our chain more than lire minutes before Ethelwyn, who had hitherto been ice itself, sprang from her chair and pointed up the stairs. "1 told you so," she cried, turning angry eyes upon her lover and myself. "I knew ho , had come home, and that you were not telling me the truth." The wretched Bob was descending to greet his family, and in the fuss that followed our excuses that we had only meant it for a joke passed Unnoticed. "But, tny dear boy," said his mother, "in your letter you told me that you were not coming home for a week." The eat was coming out of the bag now, unless he bad the sense to see what he ought to do. Not he! "1 didn't write any letter, mother," he blurted out. "I meant to write when 1 got to town." "But I found one ',milled under my bedroom dour," she said, as if she did not understand. "Well, I didn't write it," the fatuous idiot repeated, doggedly. "Somebody's been playing a joke upon you." Ethelwyn glanced from her lover to myself, and I'm afraid we both quailed before her. I'm perfectly sure at least that I did. How on earth Was it all going to end? I felt inclined to throw up my hand and let the game play itself out in Bei own way. It wag nearly half-an-hour before Norris and I could get that wretched young man to our- selves, and then we expended upon him all the vials of our wrath "1 couldn't help it," he answered, feebly. "I met old Gregory, the housekeeper, on the stain, and as she would have told mother 1 thought it best to come 4 sit. But, look here, what does all this fuss mean ? What have 1 done that you should want me to hide?" "Heaven help you! You know as well as I do," I said, amazed at his callousness, "what you have done. You have brought the greatest dis- grace a man can bring down upon his family, and, to be plain with you, it won't be your own fault if you don't pay for it with your life.' "But—but—" he stammered, trembling in every limb, "I have done nothing wrong. 1 know I got into trouble with that woman, and that she tried to blackmail me on a charge of forging a man's name to a cheque, and said she would come and tell the governor if I did not buy her off. I've managed to raise the money to square her; that was why I went up to tow? and why I came back so quickly." A light was beginning to dawn upon me. "Did you gee this woman?" !corns. .Did she come here?" "Yes, the night before last in the park. She said she would call at the house in the morning if I did not let her have the money. So off I bolted to town to see what I could do." "Will you wear by all you hold sacred that you did not lay hands upon that woman ?" "I swear it! At that moment the deer ef the sited.. was thrown open, and the squire stored is • state or great excitement with a newepaper in his hands. "My good friends," he began, "this evenieg's paper from York has just been brought to me by the police. It contains terrible news. A poor wretch committed suicide in that city this morn- ing. leaving behind him a confessien that on the

night before loot he murdered his wife by atrang- ling her in my park. It appears he had reason to believe that she intended running away with a man. The sergeant and three constables are waiting outside to aearch the park." For a moment I thought I should become hysterical. I dared not look at the Major, and he confessed afterwards that he dared not look at me. As for young Bob, I don't know how he felt. Very much like a murderer who is reprieved at the laat moment. It was touch-and- go work for him. They searched the park, but found nothing. Then someone suggested the ice-house. I believe It was the Major. Here ends the story. Heaven be thanked it had a happier termination than I thought at one time it would have.

Norris sold out of the Army after him marriage, and I am proud to say I am godfather to him eldest son, a promising young reseal of three now. lam also glad to any that young Bob—as I shall always call him—is to-day quite a reformed character. In a great measure I think he owes it to the fright he received in con- nection with the little matter which forms this story. To this day I always avoid that ice-house. If I were Sir Robert I should pull it down, The Major quite agrees with me. 0:11E EN.]

LAND VALUES AND FRIENDLY SOCIETIES.

In considering how land value. taxation will affect Friendly Societies it is first of all necessary to get clear as to the functions these associations should perform in society. The accepted idea of the functions of a Friendly Society is that its operations should be carried out so as to secure to its members, drawn almostentirely from the working classes, the I greatest possible benefit in the matter of insurance spinet death, old age, accident and sickness. A great deal of the opposition offered by supporters of friendly societies to the taxation of land values arises through Abele enpporters looking at the question in the light of the societies being landowners pure and simple. Other interests or duties are overlooked or but partially considered. Many of these societies have their funds invested partly, some in greater degree than others, in ground rents. Very seldom, if ever, are tie whole of the funds invented in this way. It follows then that some part of the funds, probably a good proportion, is invested in other ways, in building property or industrial concerns. The land values proposal is to transfer all present rates and taxes to a land value basis. The present system of taxation penalises land users in proportion to their enterprise or industry and hits the accisty through the building property or the indoetrial enterprises in which their funds are invested. Land Values Taxation means taxes accord- ing to the value attaching to land by reason of the presence and activity of population,irrespective of the nee to which it is put. This means the relief of buildings and industry, and friendly societies will benefit as much an anyone by the change. Then again, the members of friendly societies would greatly benefit individually through the new system of taxation. Taxes on land values would have the effect of bringing much valuable and productive land that is at present idle into use, and of encouraging the better development of badly used land. The resultant increased demand for labour would tend to banish unemployment and would raise wages. Surely this is a matter of great importance end advantage to members of friendly accietics. The gain to the members as householders and wage-earners would be enormous, esen if it were cert tin, which it is not, that the eucieties' investments would suffer. SIDNEY J. PHILLIPS. '

AS YOU GO THROUGH LIFE.

Don't look for the flawa as you go through life; And even when you find them, lii. wise and kind to be somewhat blind, And look for the virtue behind them; For the cloudiest night has • tint of light Somewhere in its shadows hiding; It's better by far to hunt for a star, Than the spots on the sun abiding.

The current of life runs ever away To the bosom of Clod's great ocean. Don't act your force 'gainat the river's course, And think to alter its motion. Don't waste a curse on the universe, Remember, it lived before you; Don't butt at the storm with your piny form, But bend sad let it go o'er you.

The world will never adjust itself To miit your whims to the letter, Some things must go wrong your whole life long, And the sooner you know it the better. It is folly to light with the Infinite, And go under at Isst in the wrestle. The wiser man shams into God's plan, As water shapes into a vessel.

Ella Whaler 11-11,

INTREPID PHUTOGRAPHERS.

Photo] 'Burrell. TWO MEMBERS OF THE WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY EMERGING FROM THE SMOKY BLACKNESS OF DUTTON TUNNEL.

On the occasion of the recent trip in a canal boat from Dutton to Marbury, only two out of about 20 members of the Warrington Photographic Society were courageous enough to venture through the Dutton Tunnel, which is a mile in length. These intrepid photographers were Messrs. J. H. Kertland and W. R. Jackson, who aver that they fully enjoyed their novel experience though at times the darkness was intense. Half way they had reason to complain of the thick smoke which made the air stifling.

kaulTEI) CHEQUES.

When is banker must recto? to honour a cheque is (1) olija he has natiee of the death of the customer; (21 ulieu lie has notice of the customer's bankruptcy. There have been 1,11110 interesting eases in connection with the first of these points. Mr. X Entrie, • rich old man, wax very ill. It occurred to him that he had not provided is his will for Mini Farcwon. So he sent for the lady at elevrn o'clock one night, wrote out a cheque for .41,000 in her name, and handed it to her. But at twelve o'clock the some night the old gentleman died, and his relations who were in the house sent word to the bank at once. Consequently, when Miss Farewon appeared at the counter the next morning and asked for £l,OOO in notes and . gold, she was politely informed that the bank could not pay the cheque. The lady I was in this position, Her cheque, as such, was waste paper, and she could not compel the executors to pay, because it was merely an intention to give; and a promise to give is of no value in English law, because it ia without valuable consideration. • • • COUNTERMANDED BY CUSTOMER.

(3) When the customer countermands • cheque, the banker must not pay it. If he doe,. he will be obliged to refuna the money to the customer. It makes not the slightest difference, as far as the banker is concerned, whether the customer had a good reimon, a bad reason, or no reason at all for ordering the draft not to be honoured. Sufficient it in that it has been "stopped," or counter- manded. If the holder t,f the cheque has any grievance by reason of the countermand, ho must settle it with the customer. • • • TRUST MONEY.

(4) A banker munt not pay n cheque if be. knows—"knows," . mind; not "su.peetn "

that it is drawn for the purpoee of 'commit- ting a fraud. Thus, Fumme had two bank. ing accounte at the same bank--one his own private account, which was overdrawn; the other wee known to the bank to be a trust account. Ile drew a cheque on the trust account to repay the overdraft on his private account; in other words, paid a private debt with money known by the hank not to be his own. The bank was compelled to make mei- tution to the trust account.

Here is a bit of advice, Never send away by post a cheque payable to bearer, unless you cross it and mark it "not negotiable" or "payee's account." Always use an "order ' cheque-book. • • •

I 0 U 'S. Many, if not most, people have either given or taken an I 0 U. Most people imagine that an 1 0 U is akin to a promissory note. This idea is another of the haw.; crop of popular delusions on legal subjects. ' It is somewhat curious that although II 0 U's are so largely in use, very few people ' know the simple !act that any departure from the very simplest form of the document in question is liable to turn it into something other than an I 0 U. and to alter its legal aspects altogether. First of all let us give the proper form of I 0 U. It is merely tbis:— To John Jones, Esq. March 12, 1897, 1 0 U Ten Pounds. £lO. BERRY F. BROWN.

No stamp in necessary. But if you write, an I have often seen, "I 0 U £lO, which I will pay in three months," or something of that kind. you require a stamp, because this is not • true I 0 U. For an I 0 U ought merely to be an acknowledgment that money is owing—not a promise or contract to pay money. An acknowledgment of indebtedness requires no stamp—a contract does. And, again, it is c..ry easy to turn an intended I 0 U into a promissory note. You do not, .and cannot, transfer the right to a debt by transferring an I 0 U for it. Thum in the above case, if John Jones sells the 1 0 U to you, you do not thereby become entitled to demand the £lO from Henry F. Brown. And if an I 0 U in not transferable, still lesa is it negotiable tui Bills and Note. are. An I 0 U been its virtue (in England) in six yearn from the time it was given. And finally, it has not the same force in a court as a Bill or Note; for a Bill or Note is, in itself, by mercantile custom, a contract; and value is presumed to have been given for it, while your I 0 U has exactly the same legal value as, and no more than, • letter aaying, "Dear Jones. know that I owe

THE EXAMINER.

SAYURDAY, JULY 1 1•11.

The short truce of THE Coronation week was LANSDOWNE scarcely over before AMENDMENT. political controversy

was revived in its acutest form. The official Opposition amend- ment to the Parliament Bill, which was tabled by Lord LANSDOWNE on Monday, is al remarkable production when it is considered that, by passing without a division the second Reading of the Bill, the House of Lords had nominally accepted it in principle. If the amendment is to be regarded as embodying the real intentions of the House of Lords, it is obvious that they are totally and irreconcilably opposed to the principle that the House of Commons should have the last word in legislation, although that principle has been endorsed by the .electors at two successive General Elections.

The amendment limits the operation of the Bill to such an extent that it would leave the House of Commons, if anything, more under lhe thumb of the hereditary Chamber than at present. It would exclude from the opera- tion of the Bill (a) any measure affecting the existence of the Crown or the Protestant Succession ; (b) any measure establishing a National Parliament, Assembly, or Council, with legislative powers, in Ireland, Scotland, Wales or England ; (c) any measure which the House of Lords takes exception to, if a Joint Committee decide that it " raises an issue of great gravity, upon which the judg- ment of the country has not been sufficiently ascertained." Such a measure would have to be referred to the electors" in manner to be , hereafter provided by an Act of Parliament." The constitution which is contemplated for •the Joint Committee in Lord LANSDOWNE'S Amendment is indicated in another amend- -ment, fathered by the Earl of CROMER. It would consist of seven Peers, chosen by the Lord CHANCELLOR, and seven members of the House of Commons, chosen by the SPEAKER, while the SPEAKER, with only a .casting vote, would preside.

_ . If Lord LANSDOWNE has any sense of humour one can well imagine him chuckling at the obstacle race which he has designed for Liberal legislation. He proposes that, before the Parliament Bill could come into Operation, two more constitutional measures must be passed, one creating a Joint Committee to which the Lords can refer any measures they think fit; another setting up a referendum. Then, when an important Bill has been passed through the House of Commons after exhaustive discussion, the Lords will have the option of sending it for trial before a Joint Committee, fifty per cent. of which will be drawn from their own body. That is, unless it proposes to relieve the con- gestion of Parliamentary business by devolution, in which case they will proceed to pass sentence on it "without the option." But with all other measures the Lords will be at liberty to hale them before this novel, Peer-packed tribunal, and the tribunal will be at liberty to order the Bill to be "referred" to the electors, with the result, of course, if the referendum is unfavourable, that the Government would practically be forced to resign. Thus, the Lords are once more, though in a roundabout fashion, putting forward their innovating claim to turn out a Government at their discretion, the very claim which produced the demand asserted by the people at two General Elections, for drastic limitation of the powers of the House of Lords. Once again, therefore, we are back at the same point—that all this talk about Reform does not mean zeal for Reform but hostility to the Parliament Bill. That is intelligible enough, but it is idle to mask that objection I by pretending that Mr. Asourrit ought now to propound proposals for Reform. The vital difference between the two parties is con- cerned with the powers of the Second Chamber, and the misfortune for the Unionist party is that the Liberal plan has been submitted to the country and approved by it. That is why the Parliament Bill must—and will—become law.

Tariff Reformers often OUR 'ORE-SIDED state that Cobden FREE TRADE." "based his whole argu-.

ment " for Free Trit..a.t; on the expectation that if we adopted it other countries would very soon follow our example. This is grossly incorrect. It is true Cobden's belief in the irresistible logic of Free Trade was so profound that he declared that other nations would speedily -follow our example. But at Aberdeen, in January, 1844, dealing with the then current argument of Protectionists that England must not adopt Free Trade unless and until other countries adopted it, he said : "The greater the restrictions in other countries, the more necessity was there for Free Trade in this." Peel said-the same thing "The best way to fight hostile tariffs is by Free Trade." The attempt to find in Cobden an authority for adoptim; Tariff Reform thus falls to the ground. But the Tariff Reformer uses the same argument when he asserts, either in appealing to Cobden or sneering at him, that " one-sided Free Trade" as he calls it, is a mistaken policy. " Protection all round" one gathers from this admis-

sion, is a very bad alternative, but to remain a Free Trade country in a Protectionist world is, it is added, suicidal. In order to fit this theory into the facts of our rapid progress after 1860 it has to be allowed that it has not always been suicidal. "For something like 25 or3o years after Free Tra6e was preached and adopted, there is no doubt whatsoever in my mind," said Mr. Chamberlain at Greenock (October 17th, 1903)" that it was a good policy for this country." Exactly when or why it became a bad policy does not appear. Nevertheless, one-sided Free Trade is now asserted to be dangerous, as well as ludicrous. When asked why, the Tariff Reformer draws a vivid picture of the Tariff walls round other countries, growing steadily higher and higher, restricting our markets more and more, until, if we are not careful, the time will come when our export trade will have been killed right off. What, on his own show- ing, there is to be alarmed at in this is not clear, since the process of killing off exports would also kill off imports, a thing he claims to be most anxious to do. As, however, the plain fact is that, notwithstanding the hostile tariffs of the world, our exports go up by leaps and bounds, there is evidently something very advantageous in our " one- sided " Free Trade.

The remarkably cordial MR. CROSFIELD'S welcome accorded to BUSY WEEK. Mr. A. H. Crosfield on Coronation Day when he appeared in the front rank of the civic procession was the subject of general com- ment in the town. Mr.Crosfield was naturally delighted with the warmth of the greeting from his old friends and constituents, and was afterwards heard toexpress his gratitude to those who treat "an unhorsed knight" —as he quaintly described himself—with such great kindness. In a way,Mr. Crosfield's welcome here was a fitting reward for the sacrifices he had made to take part in the celebrations at Warrington. On the eve of the Coronation Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield were present at the distinguished reception given by Lord and Lady Derby at Derby House, and Mr. Crosfield had to hurry away to catch the night train north. On Sunday Mr. end Mrs. Crosfield attended the " At Home" given by Mr. and Mrs. Rothschild at Gunners- bury Park,and later in the week were present at an important meeting of the British Section of the International Association for Labour Legislation, which was presided over by Colonel Seeley, and addressed by the Prime Minister of Australia, Sir John Finlay of New Zealand, and Mr. Ramsoy Macdonald, M.P. Last evening Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield were dining with Prince Albert of Schleswig- Holstein at a party given in his honour by Lady St. Helier. The Prince is Hon. President of the Swiss and Central Europe Golf Association, of which Mr. Crosfield is the Acting President. The Association is one which does a great deal to bring people of different nationalities into contact, helps to break down racial prejudices, and forwards considerably the cau-, of sane Inter- , nationalism.

One of the best cartoons which have appeared from the pen of " F.C.G." for some time was published on Wednesday. It depicts the shade of Lord Salisbury standing on the brink of an abyss and warning back the 'impetuous martial form of /Lord Lansdowne, who bears the sword of Veto in his right hand. Beneath the drawing is the following significant dialogue: The Spirit of Lord Salisbury: "Whither, my lord? Lord Lansdowne: I go to throw myself into the breach!

The Spirit: Beware! This is a gulf, not breach!

Preaching at Black-burn on Sunday, the Hey. Silas K. Hocking made some interesting references to present-day greed and discon- tentedness. There was, he said, no place in England where they could see so many dis- contented faces as in Hyde Park on a summer's afternoon. If they went there they would see people driving in their splendid carriages and motor Cars. Some of them would be dukes, earls, knights, etc. Here they would see, not the poor people,but the idle people, who had the word discon- tentedness written on their faces. He (the speaker) had studied their faces time after time, and he had been always depressed with the number of discontented faces. The happiest people in the world were those whose lives were spent in some useful employment. • • _ _

In the course of an important and sugges- tive report on various phases of local elementary education, Mr. .1. Moore Murray (director of education) alluded to the present day tendency for indulgence in pleasure as greatly affecting the work in the schools. There can be little doubt that the springing up of picture palaces and other cheap entertainment houses must have an ' effect on the capacity and inclination of the rising generation for day school work. Mr. Murray suggests the re-introduction of home-work as a counter- move, and it will be interesting to see how the proposal is received. There are strong arguments, educationally, for giving the scholars some home task which will carry forward the teaching of the daytime without being a burden to young minds; and further- more it has become necessary that " the craze for pleasure' should receive the serious attention of all who are concerned in the training of the _ youth of the town.

We are indebted to 'Mary Lady Gerard for a very entertaining account of the Coronation given at a gathering at Garswood Hall on Saturday night. She started at 6.30 a.m., and took two hours to drive a mile, the distance of her house from the Abbey. " When I got near the Abbey it was nearly nine o'clock, the time appointed for closing the doors, and I did not know what to do. I thought that, perhaps, like one &the foolish virgins, I should be left out. However, a friendly policeman told me I had better get out of my carriage, and in my heavy robes I walked about 100 yards into the Abbey. I did not get a very good place ; I am not a ducness—only a baroness—and I got put up rather high in the transept." She did not get home again until 6 p.m., having break- fasted at 4.30 a.m.

• • Her Ladyship then went on to say: We all illuminated our houses, because there is one thing a London crowd never forget., and if you do not illuminate your house on a great occasion like this they sill break your windows. They like to have a show or some- thing to amuse them. The crowds were grand. I movcd among them, and I scarcely saw a man who was the woree for liquor. Aid I must pay my ttibute to the London police. They performed their duties in • moat marvell,us manner under very trying conditions. The lady does herself an injustice, we are' sure, in suggesting that she illuminated her house to save her windows. Her fears are out-of-date, though it is true that once upon a time she would have been quite justified in fearing the crowd's resentment at an unilluminated house. The " Westminster Gazette" recalls the case of a Constable of Westminster who was out of town when a quite unimportant British victory was being celebrated, and who came back to find his house burnt down by an indignant mob.
