NOTES AND NOTIONS.

"Very voluntnious" was the description applied to the minutes of a certain committee by one of our local legislators at a meeting this week. 1,51.

It is estimated that the American con- sumers pay to the sugar refiners in the United States about 100,000,000 dollars lannually as tariff profits.

Old Loyalist (who has allowed the" staff" to go to the procession to see the King and Queen).—" Well, I hope you saw them well, Mary ? " "Yes, thank you, mum, they looked lovely. The one from our street 'ad on a new tunic and 'is medal all shined up.' Punch. _ .

Three features of tlie Coronation celebra- tions in Warrington have been a credit to the organisers—viz., the treats to the old folks, the Workhouse inmates, and the cripples. The various participants—whose opportunities for enjoyment are so limited— spent a delightful time amidst fresh and agreeable surroundings.

Mr. Lloyd George,-addressing a Wesleyan congregation in London, on Sunday, said religion must be the inspiration of states- manship. The mistake the Labour party were making was in thinking they would win reform through the people who suffered only, but no reform was ever obtained except by the help of those who profited nothing, and even those who lost personally.

• The tramways manager (Mr. J. Telfer) has presented a report on the renewal of plant, in which he mentions the re-laying of the track as the most serious item, and the one for which special provision will have to be made. Mr. Telfer estimates that the cost of providing new rails &c. and re-laying the I track, will work out at £3,000 per mile of single track. This means that £2OO a year per mile on eight miles of track should be added to the renewal fund—a total ot £1,600 per year. • •

The suggestion has been made that there will be a reluctance to create Peers for the purpose of carrying the Parliament Bill owing to the recent Coronation creations. History, however, does not support this supposition. William IV. was crowned at Westminster on September 8, 1831, the Reform Bill being passed in the House of Commons on September 11. On October 7 it was rejected in the House of Lords on the second reading by a majority of forty-nine. The Government were not deterred by the fact that the Coronation was so recent from taking such steps as placed the Reform Bill on the Statute Book. o o Lord Carrington, the Minister of Agricul- ture, is never tired of addressing words of counsel and encouragement to small holders of agricultural land. Experience has shown, he says, that if they go about their work in the right way they can make a decent living on five or six acres. By adopting intensive methods of growing the hest paying crops they have a chance of securing a return of £lO or £l5 an acre. The demand for the produce they supply is growing every day, and if the Englishman does not meet it the foreigner will.

• • Cremation still makss but slow progress, Ithe vast majority of people preferring the older modes of burial. The " Hospital" says that though the practice of cremation seems destined to increase extensively, both from its hygienic advantages and from the con- tinual and unsatisfied demands in every urban centre for new places of burial, it is surprising to find that in the United Kingdom last year there were only 840 cremations. lOur contemporary thinks that if one example were set in the matter of Royal obsequies it might be followed and cremation come into greater favour.

THE PAST AND THE PRESENT. THE CONDITIONS OF THE PEOPLE.

[By B. EngIBC

It is only by looking into history and comparing the Past with the Pretest that we can arrive at a decition as to whether the conditions of the people have progressed or retrograded. Let us for example take the reign of George 111. and see how and what were the conditions .of the people at that time.

When George 111, wan crowned there were lean than 8,000,0(X) of people in England. The workpeople had no vote or v. ice in the nothing of the laws ; they were not allowed to organise on Trade Union lines. 'I hey were illiterate, uneducated and ill-fed. As the days of George Ill's. reign proceeded they brought bard times to the common people, they brcught an eid to many of England's green fields, cool w, ode, wooden ships, dusty windmillecumberous waterwheels, they put an end altogether to merrie England of the 16th and 17th centuries and gave factories, ugly streets and insanitary houses instead. Injustice and tyranny over the commoner remained —the commoners who were driven like sheep from the pastures into the new factorieewbich gradually were erected. There they worked 14 and 15 hour, a day, there they were imprisoned, adults tortured and children whipped, in many cases the mill doors were kept locked all day no that no one could get out, the windows were kept closed,the air inside was hot and poisonous. Instances are recorded that when they fell ill they were fined for each day absent to pay for steam, rent, and light- ing of the mill. This in what they had been brought, to before the end of the reign of George I ll. The common people were sick and ignorant, the richer classes were greedy and tyrannous, England wan then a nation of amen masters with mall and aelfiah minds. Society was Ins state of open vice, it was a weakness to be virtuous, men who did not drink till they were help- lessly drunk were treated with contempt, reformers were reviled, it aas gentlemanly and ladylike to swear, everybody wbo was anybody was expected to colour the Engl.& language with profanity—even clergymen swore. Barrels of beer were not infrequently entered into the accounts of the church and chapel choirs and no doubt they rang lustily, if not always intone. The aristocrata and the autocrats then held the power. Government and the powers of lawmaking were in the hands of a few.

Now in the day. when George V. is crowned we see a mighty change and • change which is vastly superior to the times of George IV. Within four years of George IV's. death workmen's combinations were legalised and later on Trade Unions were gradually formed. The Flower of steam had practically revolutionised trade and assitted more than anything else in bringing ourselves into rapid communication with moat of the people of the earth and enable the i,eople of these lands to partake of and enjoy the fruits and foods of other c untrim The workers, as a rule, do not now work more than nine hours • day as against II in the days cf George IV.—and obtain more than double the wages. Food and fruits of all kinds are of much greater abundance and variety and most are now within the reach of all classes. The people are better housed and clothed—altogether they have more comforts in life. The spirit of the age is more religious and humanitarian. Now democracy has raised its head and claims the power to govern. The vote is almost within the reach of all. The poor, the maimed and the sick are now cared for. Educa- tion is free, and all now receive it. Probably the moot humane Act were passed viz., the Old Age Pensions Act, is now on the Statute Book. What the end of the reign of George V. mid see it is difficult to forecast,but that the march of the people d.espite strife and struggle is ever upward and onward along the path of progress,no one who is consistent with history ancient or modern can deny. There is implanted in man a spirit which nothing can quench and it will never be satisfied until it roaches its full fruition into the Golden Age.

FUNNY GOLF STORIES.

Not very long ago, one. of be popular monthly magazine.; secured an interesting collection of these, and Mr. Balfour contributed the following as the drollest all-round anecdote be has ever heard :—Two players determined to play a match by moonlight. The antagonists were in every respect worthy of each other, and as the match proceeded fortune did not appear to incline to either side. At last they canoe to a long bole, and to each it occurred *Ulm same time that a critical moment had been readfred, aid that it was tecewsery to adopt heroic measures. They drove off two long bane, which to the eyes of the ordinary lia raj ian pageearsre:lif tivorwishjinotwo.nigittt, f:arcliroyfolde combatants declared that he saw perfectly where his ball bad pne, and they walked off with unfaltering steps in the direction of the hole. When they had gone about 180 yards neither begen to show any signs of indicating that he had reached the place where he expected to find his ball. Both went on with unhesitating stride At last, when they got to the putting green, aome bundled yards or no beyond the longest recorded drive, both balls were found lying within a clab's length of the hole. Each Iplayer had arranged to drop a ball through a hole in

his-trouser's pocket ! Sir Henry Seton-Karr, CMG., has "gone one better " than this. I well remember bow, writing in • similar üblication on a different part of the same subject, he remarked that occasionally even ordinary players can do a bole in two, or even one, but never in a serious com- petition. He added an mousing story of one such, which he said he had read of a competition on a certain course where there was a pa: Beeler hole whose green could be reached by any ordinary driver in one. It was a blind holo, bounded by high gorse, hut otherwise poseeseing no peculiar or uncommon features. A certain mischievous lassie, having con- cealed hermit in the gorse close to the green, pro- ceeded, during the course of the competition, to mark every drive that reached the green, and then darting out of her concealment and back before the pta}ere came in sight, droppedeach ball no driven into the hole, but not more than one of each couple, if perchance, both players bad reached the green. In every case the owner of the ball, only too ready to believe in his own skill or luck, claimed the hole in one stroke, naturally believing he had done it. When the Cr turns came dropping in, the trouble began. Major Bunker, the first happy arrival, atmounced in stentorian tones that he had done such a hole in one. Colonel Niblick soon rifter, in similar manner, proclaimed a like result. The feelings of the club generally may be better imagined then described when at least half-a dozen players, one after another, unwillingly to hide succesMul achievement, triumphantly or modestly, as the cane might I*, returned their respective cards, duly attested, showing in each case this particular hole done in one stroke. The memory of that particular golf competition atill survives ; but the remarkable coincidence of at least six players doing the same hole in one stroke in the some day has not yet been explained to the satisfac- tion of that particular club committee. Truly a certain limits bad much to answer for. HOMING.

The record. of pigeon-flying in connection with I Sankey Bridges Homing Society, remind me that neither is this anything like a new thing under the sun. Ass matter of fact, the ancienta were extra- ordinarily clever and successful in training "homers " —not pigeons only, but even such birds as swallows. In proof of this, one has merely to turn to Montaigne's essays. In the one "Of Poets " this occurs : " Cecinna'a invention to send back new. to his family was quick, for he took swallows along with Idol from home, and turned them out towards their nests when he would send back any news; setting a mark of some colour upon them to signifie his meaning, accord- ing to what he and his people bad before agreed upon. lAt the theatre at Rome, maaters of families carried pigeons in their bosoms, to which they tyed letters, when they had a mind to send orders to their people at home ; and that the pigeons were trained up to bring back an answer." An expert has been at some pains to show that the ancients beat modern trainers in that they succeeded in educating birds to go to and fro as aerial messengers. It is po.nted out that Gibbon, in his version of the siege of Acre in 1199-1:91 (" Decline and Fall ef the Roman

Cmpire," Chap. 59), writes : —"Nine battles, not unworthy of the name, were fought in the neighbour- heal of Mount Carmel, with such •icissitude of fortune that in one attack tbe Sultan forced his way into the city ; that in one sally the Christi.na penetrated to the Royal tent. By the means of divers pigeons a regular correspondence was maintained with the besieged." The same historian, in his account cf St Lcruis. heroic assault and capture of Damietta (l 219 writes :—" Under the eye of their intrepid monarch, the barnto and knights of France displayed their invincible contempt of danger and discipline ; his brother, the Count of Artois, etormed with incon- siderate valour the town of MaSSOUra, arid the earlier pigeons announced to the inhabitanm of Cairo that all was lost." The employment of carrier pigeons is frequently mentioned in the Elisabethan drama.. CORONATION AMENITIES. LIBERALISM.

Reader, of Lod Morley's " Compromi•e " will remember the pissags in which he deplored the disregard of principles in the polities of the time in which that hook wan written. Many yearn have passed 'dace then, yet it is doubtful if people have learned that without principlen politics become. • mere ecramble for office and the personal benefits whieh self interest prompts men to seek. It is particularly to be regretted that Liberal., in the past. have laid tbernmelves open to this charge of neglect- ing Ow theory of their policy, for the message of Liberalism in no inspiring and full of such hopefulness for the future, that it mliould command the support of the great majority of the people, and not be hable to attack by its optonents on the ground that, it is loco.. rese 4 with its avowed principles and is a departure frm the past po'icy of the party. The prement is a moat opportune time for • preen- lotion of the principles and tolicy of Liberalism in a suitable form and at a price which brings it within the reach of all. Such a presentation is given in Prof. L. 'l'. Holahouse's little book, "Liberalism," publieherl in the " Boma University Library," by Messrs. Williams and Norgate at the price of one shilling. It is no mere sketch of the present pro- posals of the Liberal party, but • calm and finely reasoned statement of the fundamental conceptions of Liberalism, and the methode propeed by its adherents for their realisation. It cannot be auggeeted that there in no need for such a book at the present Juncture when it in borne in mind that one of the commonest objectionn to the measures which form together a coherent and consistent ',they raised by opposing parties, and even by some of the older Liberals, is that they are, in principle, opposed to the Liberalism of the days of Cobden. Bright and Glad- mono, and form in reality the first instalments of thorough going eocialistie legislation Jointed on an unwilling party by men who are trait re to their traditions and unworthy of the name of statesmen. These obj redone to the great anietiorative social measures, which are being urged upon the country by the present Liberal administration, are based on an imperfect understanding of the meaning of liberty. It is, therefore, appropriate that Mr. Hohbouse should devote a large share of his attention to tracing the continuity of the new Liberalism with the old and this he shows to be in an enlarged conception of liberty. individual and social. The older Liberals had aimed at enuring liberty for the individual ; but they conceived that this could only he achieved by limiting the activity of the State as far as possible. Personal freedom, they thought, won incompatible with the wide powers claimed for Governments. The new Liberslimm is as emphatic an the old in its demand for liberty, but its conceptioa of this is wider, and based on deeper analysis. Liberty in not to be conceived as the right of a man to follow any course of conduct he chooses regardless of its effects on others; that form of liberty in found nowhere in the world. It is to be regarded es the right of a man to the fullest power of self•develop- ment, only in in so far as that does not hamper and retard the development of bin fellows. Liberty there- fore, implies restraint and equality, for it is imposeible that true liberty should co-exist with privilege. The freedom that the Liberalism of the twentieth century strives to secure in the equal freedom of all for the full development of human personality, and this freedom is bleed on mutual restraint. It is recognized that this cannot be obtained by allowing free-play to all the desires of man. As Mr. flobhouse mays, Social freedom rests on restraint. Iris • freedm that can be enjojed by all the members of • community, and it in the freedom to drools among those lines of activity which do not involve injury to others. . . . . . But, inasmuch as injury inflicted in itself crippling to the sufferer, a. it lowers his health, confines hie life, cramps bin powers, no the prevention of such injury sets him free. The restraint of the apgremeor is the freedom of the sufferer and only by restraint on the actions by which men injare one another do they as a whole community gain frmerlorn in all menses of conduct that can be persuett without modal djAgrmony." Tile policy of then:raw Liberal:Bm is jostilied, there- fore, on the ground that the coercive power exercised by the Slate yields a more effective freedom to the Community, individually and collectively. This is the test of each proposal for Stale inter- ference, a test wlich must be met. For it is of the essence of Liberalism in its extension of the sphere of governmental control that if should create more liberty than it curtails : that each of its legislative acts should 'net the bounds of freedom snider yet.' It may be said, then, that the underlying principle of modern Liberal legislation is the imposition of varieties of restraint on individuals and bodies of individuals for the good of the community as a whole, the endeavour to s,'cure an ever-widening liberty to the whole body of the State to permit of the fullest self-developntent of the individual is every direction. This can only be achieverl by the progressive externeion of the area of Governmental action, the gradual removal of privilege, and the substitution of the common will—acting for the common good in place of the unrestricted liberty of the individual in the ournit of his own private ends. The rapidity with which progress towards this goal will be made depends upon the growth of the democracy, and Liberalism seek., therefore, to secure a fuller and more effective representation of the people in the goveroment of the country. It is interesting to note that Mr. liohhouse is fovea the breaking clown of the barriers of sex. " Restrictions of sex," Ire say., "are in every reerct parallel to restrictions of class. There are doulalmsa occupatione for which the women are unfit. But if no, the test of fitness is sufficient to exclude them. TI,, 'open road for women 'in one application, and a very big one, of the' open road for talent,' and to secure them both ie of the essence of Liberalism."

- Liberalism, as expounded in this book, is seen. therefore, to be no played-out policy in the last agonies of its expiration, but la message of hope, and ever-expanding freedom, to the toiling masses of the people. Toth.ee who wi.l to have some share in the shaping of the future, no more inspiring exposition of the principles of Liberalism could be gives than Professor llobbouse's little Look.

CHAPTER XXXII,. THE KHAN'S STORY.

Miriam sat apart. I will tell you. Illness struck me down without warning. I must have fallen in a comatose condition, for the doctor pro- nounced me dead. Preparations were made for my funeral. is he had been in daily attendance on me for weeks previously, there was no question of an inquest and so I, a liv- ing man, was placed in my coffin. You can imagine my horror when the trance ended and I came to myself, shivering and famished but with all my faculties about me. I was in the act of rising from my coffin, when Miriam came in and immediately pro- cured food and wine.

For purposes of her own, she kept my re- vival a dead secret from everyone. My old friend, Prince Kolakowski had died a few hours previously." The Princess seized the Khan's hand, her eyes filling with tears.

- 'You thought, Princess," he continued, "that your husband had been condemned to Siberia, and that he wan labouring in the mines, but such was not the case. After an, important interview with the Czar noon the certain much needed reforms which be strenuously advocated, he was waylaid and brutally disfigured." "Holy mother," exclaimed the Princess, "disfigured! How?" " His enemies could not alienate the Em- peror from his faithful minister, and no to prevent an exposure of their misdeeds, they contrived to throw a bomb filled with a cor- rosive fluid in his face, and when he pre- aented himself at my residence asking for protection, which I gladly gave, he took a vow that never again should man or woman look upon his scarred and injured face. Under the same roof we lived, a bond of friendship existing between us which nothing could sever. The shock of his sudden death was, I believe, the cause of the catalepsy which followed by paralysis, which seized upon me. I was prostrated by grief. I (mind later, on partially recovering, that un- known to me, Miriam had had the body of Prince Kolskowski placed in my coffin and the lid immediately screwed down, and to rave explanations at • time when she wan distracted, he was buried under my name. NEW CANADIAN COINAGE.

The official approval of the Royal Mint authorities in London of the deeign of the new Canadian live and ten dollar gold pima. Ma been obtained, and in the autumn the new coinage will be lamed from the branch of the Royal Mint at Ottawa. They will be in size and intrinsic value at the same as the American coins of like value, and sill proiably be accepted at par in the United States, this proving a great convenience to the Canadiana who now crone the border unprovided with American money. Practically the only gold coins in one in Canada at present are the American geld piece., and the number of these in circulation is surprisingly large. At the end of last year the Reosiver-General tor Canada held no leas than sixty-eight million dollars' worth, thus ahowing the large demand there I. for gold coinage in Canada

WALKING DAY AT PENKETH.

Those who held the view that Walking Day would be a failure this year having two days' celebrations together, must have felt a relief to see the remarkable success of Friday's programme last week. Jones, M.A. with his teachers and workers followed, the route being through the village to Hood-lane, Sankey, Liverpool-road to the vicarage field, where sports, &c. were enjoyed, tea being served in the Great Sankey Sunday School, by the teachers and workers. . .

The 'Wesleyan teachers, scholars and workers, formed their procession by the chapel, headed by the Penketh Tannery Band at 2 o'clock, taking Warrington-road, Heath- road, Greystone-road, through Mrs. Dodds' drive, and then via Station-road to Penketh Lodge where a halt was made and refreshments were served by Mr. and Mrs. C. CORONATION BIRVICILA.—At St. Margret end All Hallow& Church on Sunday, epecial nerviest were held in commemoration of the Coronation. Vale Owen, read the account of the Coronation eervice from the special communion service es issued by the two Archbishop. At the end of this rending he called the attention of the congregstion to the wonderful beauty of the wording, so wonderfully beautifol indeed that it seemed alnioet beyond the unaided powers of men, and he had little deuut that it was inspired. Continuing he .aid he wished to make them clearly understand one thing. The queetion of the Diseetablishrnent of the Church in Wales and that in England went together, for they were hot two puts of one whole. Years ago he was almost, if not quite, • convert to Disestablishroent, for he had then felt it would be for the good of the nation. A. years rolled by, whether one grew wiser or no, one's opinion, came to be hued on wider information and •nperlen.. rod 1101011 g his experiencea were two Coronations. Ile wanted his hearers to understand that in cue of the Church being disestablished (for good or for evil one thing *as certain—there would not be any more Coronations. How could there Ire? Who was there to crown the King? No doubt there would be some great civic ceremony. But all the religious unction of kinship with the service of Holy Communion and the solemn anointing by God's high priest of the nation, with the solemn injunction!. the came of God to role Be people, would be gone. The Church once disestablished, all would be swept away, and there would be no Coronation nod anointing of the King to bin regal nod priestley No more of that for ever. Stockton Heath. WIDNES SHOP FIRE. THREE WOMEN RESCUED.

The Widnes Fire Brigade received early on Sunday morning a call to a shop Ore in Ann-street West, Widnes. Tbie an their third call within a fortnight. The alarm had been given by a passer-by, who shortly after nee o'clock bad noticed enrole issuing from the drapery shop of Elias Cullen. The flee had started chiefly st the rear of the premises. The occupants were aroused, but it WWI found impoesible to get them Oct with safety except by means o I ladder. Mrs. Cullen and her daughter were rescued through the front bedroom window amid drizzling rain. Tbe woman wko kept a chip busingu nest door VMS alto rescued in the same fashion.

An adequate supply of water being obtainable, the firemen's efforts were soon rewarded, and the lire was got under. Considerable damage was done to the furniture sod the shop's contents both by fire and water.

TALKS ON HEALTH.

FAIIIILT DOCTOR.]

READACFIES AND EYEGLASSES.

This week I am going to cure headsets.. so walk up, ladies and gentlemen! No one who has not got a headache need apply Suppese that out of my readers there are • hundred pepole with pato in the head. I am prepared to cure live of those hundred patieute by ordering them proper glasses to correct the astigmatism of their eyes. They must not go to a quack, nor must they buy a pair of glasses off a barrow in the erect or in a top-shop. Eyee are worth untold gold, and money spent on an eve-specialist is well spent. The hospita!s are there for the needy. It is use:ess to swallow headache powders if your eyes are all wrong. Thirty per cent. of the headaches are due to constipation. can cure all of them by impressing on the patients the importance of being trained in regular habits from early childhood, in- structing them to drink plenty of pure water, and to adopt all the other me.ures I have no often referred to in this column.

===== 0002601_19110701_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLY TO BED.

Now I have cured thirty-five out of the hon. divot. The next group of patients suffer trete headaches from overwork and want of out- door exercise and fresh air. These van be cured if they will go to bed two hours earlier for • fortnight. Their brains are weary. It is torture to get up in the morning. and the tired brains will be no grateful for the extra rent that they will never ache again. Thin applies to children who are being over- worked at school. Needless to say, the extra sleep must be taken in a roost that is well ventilated. Young ladies with headaches must be cured of their anaemia. It is taken treatment to adnlintister powerful drugs. which may deaden the headache for • brief period at the cost of poisoning ihe system. Grave the headaches alone, and treat the anaemia with a plentiful supply of good food, fresh air, sleep, sunshine, and exercise. When the anwmia ISM gone the !;esilaches will have suns too. Antemia ac- counts for the headaches in fifteen per cent. of case.. I have nearly e.t.a the whole hun- dred, hot there is a small number left whose reuralgias in the upper story are dire to Bright s disease, or some grave disease ' which cannot be cured. Such patients ere in a worse plight than the young people who can cure themselves by commonsense treat- ment. These few cams must be left to eta care of their own doctors.

GOD'S MEDICINE FOR INFLUENZA. May I suggest to some of you to join a choral society and learn how to sing? Many of you do not know how to breathe properly; you are not making the most use of your lung-power; your blood is not being no thoroughly aerated at every breath as it ought to be. Especially is this advice valu- able if you are young enough to have soft bones still, for your chest Call be developed and filled out while you are young. The time will come when your ribs are hardened nt the joints, and no more expansion can lie obtained. Please, mothers, let your delicate daughters with weak and narrow chests have singing lessons. Make them go to the open window every morning and take ten deep breaths through their noses. There is nothing like breathing exercises and singing to improve the general health. Fresh air is God's medicine; make the most of it. You are frightened of fresh air; believe me, there is more danger in riding inside a stuffy omnibus, taking in the used-up air from the other occupants of the car, than there in in riding on the top of the omnibus on a cold morning. I never ride inside- always on the top, even if it is raining. One of those ten inside passengers has influenza, or has just recovered from it, or is just going to have it; half an hour's steady breathing of influ- enza germs, and what is the result? Ask of the doctor, who smacks his lips avec an influensa epidemic.

CURED IN SPITE OF CURER.

A small rupture of the navel in children will disappear of its own accord, although it is safer to take the precaution of applying • binder with a penny sewn into it to keep the lump back. It is not at all uncommon to ace babies with ruptured navels, but it is exceedingly rare to see a child of fifteen with the name defect; they have all been spontaneously cured by Nature and the pro- cess of growth and development. Whatever you do to a rupture of the navel it will di. appear, hence the extraordinary number of -safe cures" that every old woman recom- mends. One tries a plaster and one an oint- ment ; another, sillier than the rest, gives a medicine to drink; a fourth may apply a lotion, but the truth is that no matter what method you adopt the rupture will be cured and the triumphant old woman who declares that it was her method which cured merely makes one smile in sorrow and pity.

===== 0002601_19110701_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT ABOUT NATURE?

It is as though my friends, the old women, undertook to make a boy of fifteen grow an inch in the year. All the old women of both ilexes would reconimend their nos- trums; one would wat, the boy's head with a watering-pot, another would eell him a pair of springs to put in his boots, a third would invent an apparatus made of elastic and steel to attach to his feet at night. At the end of the year, to and behold! miracle of miracles, the boy, sure enough, has grown an inch. "Them you are," ex- claim the old women, "we told you we could do it." And the boy's father writes: "It is with great pleasure that I testify to the splendid result, of the Watering-Pot Treat- meat, the Heel Spring Treatment, and the Patent HOC. POCUS Steel Boy Lengthener. They were entirely successful in the case of my son. Please make what use you like of this letter, on I am only too anxious that other parents who want their boys to grow should be able to avail theno.lves of thee* truly miraculous methods; the price for the complete course, ten guineas, is absurdly cheap." You must not say that the boy would have grown an inch anyhow, because that merely shows your ignorance.

===== 0002601_19110701_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
PREVENTION BETTER THAN CURE.

Self-help is what we all want to cultivate. The very helpless may be dependent on charity, but it must always be unsatisfac- tory, I think in my work among the poor I have met many cases where the people have been robbed of their independence and desire to fend for themselves by the indis- criminate charity that is pourea on them. Boots have been given, but they have never been polished, although it looks more re- spectable to have the boots polished, and ' also it prolongs their live. Dear me, if you only knew what a lot of illness could be pre- vented by keeping the feet dry iy good boots. Prevention is an much better than cure. The cold you get through wearing leaky helots was the beginning of your get- ting consumption. How can you keep people well whin they take no little trouble them- selves. If you give them garments, they wili not keep the buttons eewn on, even though the children thentaelvee have learnt to sew. Let is. bear in mind that more than half the illness in the world is preventehlo. and that the people must leara to help themselves,

Hard Facts for Tariff Reformers.

Sir,—Some striking figures regarding the distribu- tion of Britikh expr.rts appear in the current number of the" Investors' Review.'

At the time Mr. Chamberlain began Vs Tariff Reform propaganda, our exports to European markets showed a preponderance of It millions over NU exports to markets within the Empire. When Free- Traders pointed out the folly of sacrificing our European markets for the problematical expansion of nor Empire trade, Tariff Reformers did nut hesitate to prophesy the nsa of our European trade, and .r increasing dependence upon the Imperial trade. It is interesting to Nee now this prophecy, like every other prophecy of the Tariff Reformers, has been falsified. Whereas the preponderance of the European over the Imperial trade was It millions in 19111, the preponderance has increment to II million. is MO. The increase of our exports to foreign countries has been 125 millions, as against 88 millions to British PossesiMmt.

Tariff Reformers, who faced with awkward figures like these, sometimrs find that the significance is disguised. or err n altered, when the figures are stated in percentages. Ilut in dd., rase even that refuge is denied to them, for the increase of cur expoits to foreign countries since the Tariff Reformers began to 'make their prophecies accounin for sixty per cent. of the total increase in our exports ; while our export. to British possessions account for only forty per cent. of the total increase.

Tide is all the more baffling because during this period foreign tariff barriers— according to the Tariff Reformers--have been increasingly built up against us; while so-called " preferences " Irene ken granted by the Colonies to on. According to Tatifl Reform principles, therefore, the dirciition of our export trade ought to have been preci,uly the reverse of what has proved ti be the fact.—Yours faithfully. WM. J. RIDGWAY.

Ii 5, Church-lane. Gorton, Manchester, 24th June, 1911.

HELPING A CRIPPLE "ON BOARD."

Coronation festivities. Owners of motor I cars cheerfully responded to the appeal for their co-operation and the result was that on rhursday, over 120 cripples were taken for a run to Chester and Eaton Hall through some of the loveliest scenery which picturesque Cheshire can boast. The sight of so many of the physically defective assembling in front of Bold-stree, Church, where the procession of cars was marshalled, was full of pathos. Some of the cripples looked pale and wan, many walked with the aid of crutches,and others who could only travel in a recumbent position, were brought to the cars in invalid carriages wheeled by brothers or sisters or sympathising friends. The cars were drawn up in Palmyra-square and came up in turn to the gates in Bold-stree4 to receive their contingents of passengers. Councillor W. H. Bellian, to whose organizing genius and wonderful enthusiasm the success of the outing was in a great measure due, hurried hither and thither directing operations as chief marshal. Mr. J. Moore Murray (director of education) was prominent amongst the band of workers who assisted the cripples to their places in the cars, and, in addition to the driver, a voluntary helper was seated in each vehicle to attend to the comfort and safety of the guests, the great majority of whom were! children. The Mayor and Mayoress (Coun- cillor Dr. Joseph and Miss Joseph) were also on the scene and when everything was in readiness, they led off in the first car at 1.45. The procession made a brave show as it passed along the main streets, several of the cars sporting flags which added to the bright- ness of the scene. Best of all, the sun shone radiantly and gave every promise of an ideal afternoon—a prospect which was perfectly realized. The charming Delamere route to Chester had been chosen and this enabled the cripples to see a stretch of country the equal of which it would be hard to, find within such easy access of a manufacturing town. Cp the bill past quiet Stretton,through pleasant pastoral country to Weaverham, thence along the level Tarporley road, arched with rich, spreading foliage, to the Delamere cross roads--the cars travelled not too rapidly to allow of admiration of the scenery, but still with sufficient speed to create that pleasant, invigorating sensation of rushing through the air. Shorlty before reaching the Abbey Arms, the cars, which had been proceeding in somewhat close processional order, spread out, and the extended distance between each car greatly mitigated the dust nuisance. On reaching Kelsall hill a magnifi- cent view, backed by the Welsh mountains, was obtained and next the route lay throughl Tarvin, with its fine church, to Hoole where ' a halt was called before making an imposing entry into Chester. At Hoole Toll Bar, the Mayor was met by Alderman John Jones, of Chester, who conveyed the apologies of the Mayor of Chester for his inability to join the party. Traversing the quaint streets of " rare old Chester," the cars attracted much attention and the police considerably facili- tated their progress by the admirable way in which they regulated the traffic. The Duke of Westminster had kindly given special permission for the cars to run through the extensive and splendidly wooded grounds surrounding Eaton Hall and this concession greatly enhanced the pleasure of the journey. Hundreds of deer were brows- ing on the fields and, startled by the advent of the great array of fearful and wonderful contrivances from the outside world, many of them dashed timidly across the path in front of the cars. Next the noble ducal mansion--Eaton Hall—with its massive entrance gates excited the admiration of the visitors. A short spin brought us to the Iron Bridge which spans the silvery waters of the Dee, and here the cars were unloaded and all prepared to spend a delightful couple of hours in a truly out-of-the-world spot. In a shelter by the river-side the cripples were first entertained to an excellent tea which they thoroughly appreciated. At the close, the Rev. Walter Seed suggested that as the treat was included in the Coronation festivities, the assembly should join in sing- ing" God Save the King." The request was fervently complied with. The liev. Walter Seed then said be was sore the g.aests would wish to thank the Mayor and Mai oress kr their presence aril the Mayor fur bin kind tUouuht in including the treat in the Coronation festivities. "Do you want to thank them?" asked the rev, gentle- man, and there were Loud eLswering cries of, Yes." Well, give them three cheers as hard as you can and the voices of the cripples were raited in lusty acclaim. •

IHE NEXT OPERA

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

The autumnal operatic performances by the Warrington Musieal Society have come to be looked forward to as among the welcome first fruits of our local theatrical and musical season. It is of no little public interest therefore to announce as, by the courtesy of Mr. Bond, the society's hon. secretary, we are able to do to-day, that the subject of the society's next venture is the Chinese Musical Comedy, "San Toy," one of the greatest successes, it will be remembered, associated with the name of Mr. George Edwardes, and probably the brightest of the brilliant series of similar works which have distinguished Daly's Theatre during the past 20 years. And though "San Toy " cannot, from some points of view, be considered quite worthy to rank with the numerous and high- standing examples of genuine light opera which now grace the annals of the Musical Society's operatic section,it is curious to note how univertal and how lasting has been its popularity especially in the provinces. Relying therefore upon past achievements, as proof of the ample talents to be found in the society's ranks,we must conclude that the question of selecting a work which would suit the popular taste has, this year. had great weight in the society's deliberations, with the result that this essay into the lighter field of musical play has been decided upon—a choice which, we venture to think,' will be hailed with enthusiasm by the great majority of our townspeople who have followed fwith interest these local operatic enterprises. "San Toy" is now in full rehearsal as many who have lingered near the Cairo-street practice-room to listen to its tuneful choruses already know, Mr. F. H. Crossley being at his accustomed post as conductor and musical director, while again the genial Mr. R. W. Lomax is acting as dramatic instructor and stage manager. The date of the public performances is the week commencing October 16th, and the place the Royal Court Theatre now in course of being re-decorated and largely re-seated. The seenery,mounting and costumingpromise, we understand, to be on a most lavish and artistic scale and altogether some 70 people will be upon the stage in the ensembles. The principal characters in the very numerous cast will h taken by the following ladies and gentlemen, many of whom will be recognised as old favourities.

Death of Mr. Francis Brown.

We regret to announce tie death of Mr. Francis Brown, who passed away at his reaidence, 47, Arpley- street. on Saturday last. The deceased gentleman, who was 79 years of age, had been in delicate health for a long time and death was riot wholly unexpected. Mr. Brown took an active interest in the religious life of the town oriel was at one time surerintendetd of the Cockherige Mission Sunday school and later he held a aindlar office at Wycliffe Congrrgational Sunday school. He was • strong adherent to the Liberal cause. Nearly twenty years ago ho retired from the roeiticn ! of mill nunieger at Metiers. A rmitage and Rigliya work e, Cocliedge, on which cocasion he was the recipient of a haadsome timepiece. He was also a director of the British Workman's Public-13611w Co., 116rsemarket- street. fire funeral took place on Wednesday after- noon. A aervice, conducted by the Rev John Yonge. was held in the house. Amongst those pretent at ilie Cemetery wet e Men-rs hienege Brown. Wesleyan Cyclloi Club. The day run to floylake arranged for Walking Day was a failure an far as number, go. hot it must be repeated at some early date. If the four ways from Warrington to Liverpool that through Crouton is the easiest and by far the hest, and that through Preacot and K totty Ash the worst on account of the long tram routes at the other end, but, by combining the two we can enjoy one of the moat eate“ive View. in South Lancashire. Instead of turning ight at Rainbill Stoops, go straight on up the hid past the fine house built by Richard Evans, of colliery fame. hoot the lane jost post the house you can se• over a hundred square mi!es. From Garston to Bill Cliffs r' 004 Itv Ellesmere Port and Runcorn Bills the land it spread out like a map, and who is not delighted with the prospect reeda a deal of satisfying. The secure] left turn to " Wootton" leads ut to the north side of Halmead Park, and at the other end a left tura takes in into the high rail through Crouton, Iluyton and Roby. A very recent st,eet improvement at Broad Grten gives us now an asphalt road through the new garden city into Edge-lane, and by following Clint- road, Oladatone-road, Paddington, Brownlow Hill and Hanover-street we arrive at the dock bide with scarcely a eight of tlte terrible trams of Liverpool. The road from Birkenhead to Devoid. is rather monotonous, but the riders on Friday afternoon spent rtrn pleasaut after- noon on the promenades at lloylake and West Kirby. and then commenced a delightful round thr,tigl. Ileawall to Chester and home by thelfelsby-road • total round of 7:: mile..
